2026年 11月 19日 全9頁

Indicators Update 
2026年 9月機械受注 
製造業が堅調で7-9月期の民需（船電除く）は4四半期連続で増加

経済調査部 エコノミスト 吉井 希祐

[要約] 
◼ 2026年9月の機械受注（船電除く民需）は前月比▲0.3％と2カ月ぶりに減少し、コン
センサス（Bloomberg調査：同＋1.5％）を下回った。民需（船電除く）を 3カ月移動
平均で見ると同＋0.3％となり、内閣府は機械受注の基調判断を「持ち直しの動きがみ
られる」に据え置いた。 
◼ 製造業からの受注額は前月比＋3.9％と 2カ月連続で増加した。化学工業では大型案件
が 1件あり、全体を押し上げた。非製造業（船電除く）からの受注額は同＋1.1％と 2
カ月連続で増加した。運輸業・郵便業や情報サービス業では大型案件が 1 件ずつあり、
全体を大きく押し上げた。 
◼ 先行きの民需（船電除く）は緩やかな増加基調を辿るとみている。企業の設備投資意
欲は強く、人手不足対応のための省力化投資や、AI 関連投資などが引き続き期待され
よう。ただし、中東情勢が悪化すれば、事業環境の不確実性の高まりやエネルギー・
原材料価格の一段の高騰への懸念から、企業の設備投資への姿勢が慎重化する恐れが
ある。

【総括】船電除く民需は小幅に減少したものの、製造業・非製造業はいずれも増加 
2026年 9月の機械受注（船電除く民需）は前月比▲0.3％と 2カ月ぶりに減少し、コンセン
サス（Bloomberg調査：同＋1.5％）を下回った（図表2）。製造業は2カ月連続で増加し、非製
造業（船電除く）も 2カ月連続で増加した 0F
1（図表 3）。民需（船電除く）を 3カ月移動平均で
見ると同＋0.3％となり、内閣府は機械受注の基調判断を「持ち直しの動きがみられる」に据
え置いた。 
2026年7-9月期の民需（船電除く）は前期比＋1.2％と4四半期連続で増加したものの、6月
末時点の企業見通し（同＋4.9％）には届かなかった。製造業は同＋8.0％と 2四半期ぶりに増
加した。業種別で見ると、電気機械（同＋31.4％）が全体を大きく押し上げた。他方、非製造
業（船電除く）は同▲0.8％と2四半期連続で減少した。 
図表 2：機械受注額と名目設備投資

（注）季節調整値。太線は 3カ月移動平均。 
（出所）内閣府統計より大和総研作成 
図表 3：需要者別に見た機械受注額

（注）季節調整値。太線は 3カ月移動平均。 
（出所）内閣府統計より大和総研作成

加している理由としては、①各系列が個別に季節調整されていること、②民需（船電除く）には船舶が含まれ
ていないが、製造業には含まれていることなどが考えられる。

### Page 3

1

3 / 9

【製造業】大型案件があった化学機械を中心に増加 
2026年 9月の製造業からの受注額は前月比＋3.9％と 2カ月連続で増加した。機種別に見る
と、産業機械、電子・通信機械、工作機械、輸送機械が増加した一方、原動機・重電機は減少
した（図表 4左、大和総研による季節調整値）。業種別では、17業種中 10業種が増加した。化
学工業（同＋57.0％）では化学機械の大型案件が 1 件あり、全体を押し上げた。業務用機械
（同＋80.5％）、その他製造業（同＋24.6％）などからの受注も増加した。他方、はん用・生産
用機械（同▲14.8％）や非鉄金属（同▲49.0％）などからの受注は減少した。

【非製造業】大型案件があった運輸業・郵便業や情報サービス業が大きく押し上げる 
2026年9月の非製造業（船電除く）からの受注額は前月比＋1.1％と2カ月連続で増加した。
機種別に見ると、輸送機械、電子・通信機械、工作機械が増加した一方、産業機械、原動機・
重電機は減少した（図表 4右、大和総研による季節調整値）。業種別では、11業種中 3業種が
増加した。運輸業・郵便業（同＋51.3％）では鉄道車両、情報サービス業（同＋25.0％）では
通信機の大型案件が 1件ずつあり、全体を大きく押し上げた。他方、リース業（同▲49.4％）
や農林漁業（同▲16.5％）をはじめ、幅広い業種からの受注が減少した。

図表 4：業種別・機種別に見た機械受注額の動き

（注）季節調整は大和総研。輸送機械に船舶は含まない。非製造業の工作機械受注は少額であるため図表から
除外したが、26年9月は前月比＋10.3％であった。 
（出所）内閣府統計より大和総研作成

【外需】9件の大型案件が下支えし、3カ月ぶりに増加 
2026年 9月の外需は前月比＋6.2％と 3カ月ぶりに増加した（図表 5）。機種別では、電子・
通信機械、原動機・重電機、産業機械、工作機械が増加した一方、輸送機械は減少した（図表
6、大和総研による季節調整値）。電子・通信機械で 3件、原動機・重電機で 2件、輸送機械で
4件の合計9件の大型案件があった。 
機械受注の外需動向を地域別に見る上で参考になる工作機械受注を確認すると、9月の外需
は前月比＋2.6％と4カ月連続で増加した（日本工作機械工業会、大和総研による季節調整値、
図表 7）。 アジア（中国を除く、同＋13.9％）からの受注が好調で 1F
2、全体を押し上げた。他方、
欧州（EU＋英国、同▲15.2％）と米国（同▲3.0％）からの受注は 4 カ月ぶりに減少し、中国
（同▲0.6％）からの受注は 2カ月ぶりに減少した（図表8）。  
工作機械受注は 10月分がすでに公表されており、内需は前月比＋2.2％と 2カ月ぶりに増加
した一方、外需は同▲5.8％と5カ月ぶりに減少した。

【先行き】民需（船電除く）の緩やかな増加基調を見込むも中東情勢等が下振れリスク 
先行きの民需（船電除く）は、緩やかな増加基調を辿るとみている。日銀短観の 9月調査に
おける「生産・営業用設備判断 DI」（先行き、全規模）を見ると、製造業（▲6％pt）と非製造
業（▲7％pt）のいずれでも、設備の不足感が示されている。企業の設備投資意欲は強く、人
手不足対応のための省力化投資や、AI関連投資などが引き続き期待されよう。 
内閣府が公表した 2026年 10-12月期の見通しによれば、民需（船電除く）は前期比＋4.3％
と 5四半期連続での増加が見込まれており、企業の設備投資意欲は引き続き強いとみられる。
製造業は同＋2.6％、非製造業（船電除く）は同＋5.2％と、いずれも堅調に推移する見込みだ。 
AI関連投資に関しては、米国のハイパースケーラーと呼ばれる企業群の巨額の設備投資など
を背景に、関連製品を生産・輸出するはん用・生産用機械 2F
3や電気機械、情報通信機械などか
らの受注が増加傾向にあり、今後もこれらの業種からの受注が民需全体を押し上げるとみられ
る（図表 9）。ただし、米国では一部の AI関連企業の収益性や財務面への懸念に加え、中間選
挙の結果を受け、AIやデータセンターに対する規制強化への懸念が強まっている。こうした懸
念が現実化し、AI関連需要をけん引するハイパースケーラーの設備投資の増勢が大きく鈍化す
れば、関連する日本企業の設備投資が下振れする可能性があり、注意が必要だ 3F
4。 
中東情勢をめぐっては、ホルムズ海峡の事実上の封鎖が続くなど、依然として不透明感の強
い状況にある。情勢が悪化すれば、事業環境の不確実性の高まりやエネルギー・原材料価格の
一段の高騰への懸念から、企業の設備投資への姿勢が慎重化する恐れがある 4F
5。 
図表 9：業種別の製造業の機械受注額への寄与度

（注）製造業の受注額（季節調整値の 3カ月移動平均）の2025年1月からの変化率に対する累積寄与度。 
（出所）内閣府統計より大和総研作成

3 半導体・フラットパネルディスプレイ製造装置製造業などが含まれる。 
4 米国のハイパースケーラーの設備投資動向が世界経済に与える影響については、畑中宏仁・神田慶司「ハイ
パースケーラーの投資動向が世界経済に与える影響」（大和総研レポート、2026年11月 12日）を参照。 
5 米国の中間選挙後の原油価格高騰リスクについては、神田慶司・畑中宏仁「米中間選挙による日本経済への
影響」（大和総研レポート、2026年 11月17日）を参照。

聯-KY 總部設於加州 Fremont，3Q26 產品

主要客戶： 
主要競爭對手：

張予柔 yurou.chang@sinopac.com 
本次報告：法說會

貿聯-KY (3665 TT) 
併購綜效超預期，獲利動能再加速

永豐觀點 
併購 Interplex、新富生貢獻雙雙超預期，獲利上修循環啟動。 
 投資評價與建議 
重申買進建議：研究部本次上修貿聯財務預估：(1) Interplex 資料通訊業務
(Datacom Business)營收規模大幅超標：10 月首個完整併表月營收約新台幣 21
億元，大幅優於研究部原先預期之約 13 億元，研究部同步上修其 2026~2028 年
營收與獲利貢獻預估。(2) 新富生月營收規模自年初以來已成長逾一倍，憑藉資料
中心高階光纖跳線的製造與品質優勢，持續拓展新客戶，研究部預估其 2027 年營
收可持續倍增。(3) 產品組合轉佳、營運槓桿持續發揮：3Q26 毛利率季增 1.8 個
百點至 32.53%，且營業費用率降至約 12%，帶動營益率創 20.78%新高。綜合預
估貿聯 2026-2028 年集團營收分別為 1,074、1,659、2,161 億元(+51%、+55%、
+30%YoY)；稅後淨利 150、267、356 元(+66%、+79%、+33%YoY)，完全稀
釋後 EPS 分別為 75.25、134.07 及 178.24 元。考量 AI 供應鏈遇短期逆風，影響
評價表現，研究部將本益比調整為 18 倍，維持買進建議，目標價 2,810 元(2027
年及 2028 年之平均預估 EPS 156.16*18XPER)。

ESG 評析 
貿聯企業永續評鑑整體為 B+等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 B 等與 A 等。 
SinoPac+ ESG 評鑑系統評等

### Page 2

2

貿聯-KY 是全球領先的連接器公司之一：貿聯-KY 是全球領先的連接器公司之一：
貿聯-KY 總部設於加州 Fremont，3Q26 產品比重為資訊科技與數據傳輸 48％、
工業用 31％、車用 8％、電器 12％；若以事業別區分，HPC 占 46％、半導體設
備(SPE)占 13％、其他事業占 41％。貿聯主要核心能力，在於高技術及客製化開
發零組件製造，產品開發以多角化及差異化策略，2026/1 完成收購新富生光電
(XFS)，2026/9/23 完成收購 Interplex 集團資料通訊業務(Interplex Datacom)，
產品線由電源與數據連接延伸至光纖跳線、機殼及機櫃等精密機構件，朝 AI 機櫃
次系統解決方案供應商發展。 
3Q26 財報公布，優於市場預期，營收與獲利同創新高，營益率首度突破 20%：
3Q26 營收 278.75 億元(+19.7% QoQ、+51.4% YoY)，毛利率 32.53%，季增 1.8
個百分點；營業利益率 20.78%，季增 3.6 個百分點、年增 1.2 個百分點，為公司
首度突破 20%，獲利表現優於市場預期。成長主要來自 HPC，3Q26 HPC 營收季
增 30%、年增 61%，營收比重由 2Q26 的 42%回升至 46%，其中電源產品為成
長主力，帶動產品組合改善；半導體設備營收亦季增 11%、年增 70%，各事業全
面成長。費用方面，營收規模擴大但營業費用未等比例增加，營業費用率降至約
12%的歷史低點，顯示獲利率改善來自規模效益與費用效率，而非一次性因素，具
備延續性。稅後淨利 42.93 億元(+44.0% QoQ、+63.3% YoY)，稀釋後 EPS 21.51
元，營收、淨利與 EPS 皆創歷史新高；前三季累計 EPS 48.04 元，已超越 2025 全
年之 46.07 元。

營運現況與分析

### Page 3

3

研究部上修 2026~2028 年營收獲利預估，原因有三： 
1. Interplex 資料通訊業務(Datacom Business)營收提前兩年達標：Interplex
的資料通訊業務(Datacom Business)自 9/23 起併表，3Q26 僅貢獻 7 天營
收；10 月為首個完整併表月，帶動貿聯 10 月營收達 118.46 億元，月增 26.9%、
年增 80.0%，其中 Interplex 貢獻 6,700 萬美元(約新台幣 21 億元)。對照公司
宣布併購時揭露之年營收 3.92 億美元(月平均約 3,300 萬美元)，Interplex 月
營收已倍增，大幅優於市場預期。研究部看好併購綜效將逐步顯現：Interplex
主要供應 AI 伺服器機殼、機櫃等精密機構件，研究部認為其營收較併購時大
幅成長，主因 AI 機櫃出貨量較年初明顯放大，且併入貿聯後，Interplex 可運
用集團的資金、客戶關係與製造資源擴大接單，雙方產品整合後可提供涵蓋配
電、數據連接、機構件與液冷的機櫃次系統方案，單一 AI 機櫃之可供應產值
明顯擴大。研究部本次上修 2027、2028 年 Interplex Datacom Business 為
貿聯帶來的營收預估，分別為 268、308 億元(原預估 183、250 億元)。 
2. 新富生光通訊規模快速放大，營收占比持續提升：新富生(XFS) 專精於光纖互
連產品的設計與製造，主要產品包括單芯／雙芯跳線、光纖尾纖、多芯光纖以
及 MTP/MPO 高密度連接器，月營收規模自 2026 年初以來成長逾一倍，獲
利能力同步改善，展現規模化高精密光學互連業務的效益。資料中心對光纖組
件的品質與一致性要求遠高於一般應用，新富生在高階光纖跳線的製造經驗與
品質優勢，加上貿聯集團的客戶網絡與全球產能布局，有助其持續拓展新客戶。
隨 AI 資料中心機櫃間光纖連接需求成長，研究部預估新富生 2027 年營收可
望再翻倍增長，且其獲利率改善亦有助提升貿聯整體獲利結構。 
3. 3Q26 營益率創新高，營運槓桿持續發揮：3Q26 營業費用率約 12%，較 1H26
的 13.7%明顯下降，顯示貿聯在承接更大規模、更複雜的客戶專案時，製造、
工程與行政職能的效率同步提升，費用無須等比例增加。4Q26 起 Interplex
完整併表，營收規模再擴大，研究部預期營益率改善幅度可望大於毛利率，稅
後淨利成長幅度將高於營收。 
HPC 電源成長最強，新平台升級為下一波動能：3Q26 HPC 營收年增 61%、季增
30%，前三季累計年增 77%。隨運算功耗提升，電源產品切入規格更高的新一代
平台；數據連接方面，貿聯同時布局銅纜與光纖，因應不同傳輸距離與頻寬需求。
貿聯於 10 月 OCP Global Summit 2026 展出適用於 OCP 及 NVIDIA MGX 機櫃
之 800V/400VDC 高功率電源方案，以及 1.6T 液冷交換器 OSFP 連接器、機櫃管
理控制與漏液偵測系統，產品範圍由電源線材延伸至配電、液冷與監控。研究部認
為，隨 Vera Rubin 平台放量及 HVDC 架構導入，單一機櫃的電源產品規格與產值
將持續提升，為 2027 年 HPC 成長主軸。 
次世代光通訊布局：貿聯 2026 年先後於 OFC 展示 Optical Shuffle、於 Computex
展示 CPO 交換器托盤方案；9 月於 ECOC 2026 展示與 ams OSRAM 合作之
microVCSEL 光學互連方案，採用 850nm 二維可定址 microVCSEL 陣列，以多通
道、低速率(wide-and-slow)架構降低功耗與延遲，貿聯負責光纖陣列組裝、光學
封裝、光學測試與量產製造；10 月於 OCP Global Summit 2026 展出 CPO、
800G/1.6T 及 XPO 光收發模組、光纖陣列(FAU)與多芯光纖連接等方案。公司表
示產業未來可能並行採用多種光學架構，貿聯以製造與整合能力參與各技術路線，
不需押注單一方案。 
半導體設備成長幅度為產業兩倍以上：3Q26 半導體設備(SPE)營收年增 70%、季
增 11%，營收占比由 3Q25 的 11%提升至 13%，前三季累計年增 48%。根據國
際半導體產業協會（SEMI）於 2026 年 7 月公布之預測，全球前段晶圓製造設備

### Page 4

4

(WFE)市場 2026 年預估年增 23.1%至 1,439 億美元，貿聯 SPE 成長幅度約為產業
的兩倍；自 2023 年以來貿聯之 SPE 業務成長即大幅超越整體 WFE 市場，超額成
長主因貿聯在客戶端的角色由供應線材零組件，擴大至高階組裝、模組與次系統製
造、測試及系統整合，應用以薄膜沉積與蝕刻設備為主，微影與檢測設備為輔；隨
設備製程日益複雜，客戶傾向將更多製造工作委外，以集中資源於核心技術，貿聯
單一設備平台之內含價值隨之提升。SEMI 預估 WFE 市場 2027 年及 2028 年將分
別成長 21.8%及 14.1%，2028 年規模可望突破 2,000 億美元，主要由先進邏輯、
HBM 等先進記憶體投資帶動；公司亦表示，許多客戶對設備投資的強勁預期延伸
至 2030 年以後，且客戶正主動協助貿聯擴充產能，以支援其自身長期成長。貿聯
在 SPE 領域的投資以擴充製造產能為主，Interplex 的精密機械加工與次系統整合
能力亦可與 SPE 業務互補，為 HPC 以外的第二成長引擎。 
隨伺服器架構升級驅動產值倍增，2027~2028 年獲利成長路徑清晰，重申買進： 
研究部認為，貿聯本次財報的關鍵不只是單季獲利優於預期，而是兩項併購同時優
於預期，帶動營收基期結構性墊高：10 月營收 118.46 億元，較 3Q26 月平均約
93 億元高出約 27%。在 AI 機櫃同時需要電源、數據傳輸(銅纜與光纖)與精密機構
件的趨勢下，貿聯透過 Interplex 與新富生補齊產品版圖，單一機櫃可供應產值持
續擴大；而未來一年內，Interplex 完整併表(4Q26)、新富生營收比重提升(2027)、
Vera Rubin 平台與 HVDC 電 源 放 量 (2027~2028) 及 次 世 代 光 互 連 產 品
(2H27~2028)等成長催化劑將陸續到位，獲利上修動能明確。 
評價方面，短期因市場擔憂 GPU 產品推出節奏可能放緩，台灣 AI 供應鏈評價短
期受到壓抑。研究部認為，CSP 資本支出與現行平台訂單短期不受影響，且貿聯的
客戶與產品布局相對分散：公司採取平台中立策略，產品同時供應 GPU 與 ASIC
架構之 AI 機櫃，並服務多家雲端服務業者；銅纜與光纖、電源與機構件等產品線
亦不依賴單一技術路線；此外，半導體設備與其他事業合計仍占營收逾五成，營運
受單一平台影響的程度低於純 GPU 供應鏈。惟考量主要客戶經營層異動可能影響
新平台推出節奏，研究部於評價上給予折價，本益比由前次之 25 倍調整為 18 倍。
搭配本次上修後之 EPS，維持買進建議，目標價 2,810 元(2027 年及 2028 年之平
均預估 EPS 156.16*18XPER)。

### Page 1

2026년 10월 29일 
기업분석

SK하이닉스 (000660) 
높아지는 이익의 눈높이, 달라지는 메모리 사이클

Buy(유지) 
목표주가 3,150,000원, 현재 주가(10/28): 1,487,000원 
▶ Analyst 박준영 jyp94@hanwha.com 3772-7481 / RA 김나우 now.kim@hanwha.com 3772-7710

3Q26 Review: HBM4 출하 확대와 예상보다 강한 가격 상승 
분기 매출액 104.6조원(QoQ +32%, YoY +328%), 영업이익 83.3조원(QoQ +38%, YoY +632%)을 기록하며 시장 기대
치를 상회함. 영업이익률은 전분기 대비 약 3%p 상승한 80%로 사상 최대 분기 실적을 달성함. 특히 DRAM ASP가 전
분기 대비 20% 후반 상승하며 당사 기존 예상치인 +23%를 상회한 점에 주목할 필요가 있음. NAND ASP 역시 20% 
중반 상승하며 예상보다 강한 가격 흐름을 나타냄. DRAM ASP 상승에는 기존 DRAM의 가격 인상과 함께 3분기부터 
본격화된 HBM4 양산 및 출하 확대에 따른 제품 믹스 개선 효과가 반영됨. 서버 DDR5 판매량 역시 전분기 대비 40% 
증가하며 실적 성장을 뒷받침함. 특히 HBM4는 기존 HBM3E 대비 높은 기술적 난도를 반영한 가격으로 공급되고 있어 
향후 출하 비중 확대에 따른 추가적인 ASP 상승 효과도 기대됨. 
이번 실적에서 중요한 부분은 예상보다 높은 가격 상승폭이 2027년 실적 추정치의 상향 가능성을 높이고 있다는 점임. 3
분기 DRAM ASP가 기존 추정치를 상회한 가운데 4분기에도 HBM4 출하 확대와 기존 DRAM의 가격 상승이 이어질 
것으로 예상되기 때문임. 동사는 4분기 DRAM 출하량이 전분기 대비 20% 중반 증가할 것으로 전망했으며, HBM4 출
하량이 HBM3E를 넘어 주력 제품으로 자리 잡을 것으로 예상함. 특히 HBM4의 양산 수율이 당초 목표를 상회하고 있
으며 높은 고객 수요에 대응하기 위해 생산성 극대화에 집중하고 있다는 점도 긍정적임. HBM4 출하 확대에 따른 믹스 
개선과 기존 DRAM 가격 상승이 동시에 진행되는 가운데 HBM과 일반 DRAM의 수익성 격차 역시 유의미하게 축소
될 것으로 예상됨. 이는 특정 제품에 대한 수익성 의존도가 낮아지면서 DRAM 사업 전반의 이익 체력이 강화되고 있음
을 의미함. 메모리 산업의 높은 영업 레버리지를 고려하면 예상보다 높은 ASP와 출하량 증가는 2027년 이익 추정치의 
추가 상향으로 연결될 가능성이 높다고 판단함.

공급 부족의 장기화와 LTA 확대, 메모리 산업의 이익 구조 변화 
이번 컨퍼런스콜에서 확인된 또 다른 중요한 변화는 공급 부족의 장기화와 장기공급계약(LTA) 확대임. 동사는 첨단 공정
의 기술적 난도 상승과 신규 생산시설 구축에 소요되는 시간을 고려할 때 메모리 공급이 수요를 충족하지 못하는 환경이 
2030년까지 지속될 것으로 전망함. 특히 글로벌 주요 고객사 14곳과 장기공급계약을 체결했으며, 2027~2028년 전체 생
산능력의 50% 이상에 해당하는 물량을 확보함. HBM의 경우 2029년까지의 물량이 이미 판매 확정된 상황으로 중장기 
수요 가시성이 상당히 높아졌다고 판단함. 주목할 부분은 LTA가 단순한 공급물량 확보를 넘어 메모리 업체의 사업 구조
와 이익 안정성을 변화시키고 있다는 점임. 계약 가격은 고객 및 제품별로 차이가 있으나 합의된 범위 내에서 시장 상황
을 반영하는 구조로 운영되고 있으며 예치금 등 실질적인 계약 이행력을 높이는 장치도 포함됨. 이는 장기적인 판매물량
을 확보하면서도 업황 상승에 따른 가격 인상 효과를 일정 부분 누릴 수 있다는 점에서 긍정적임.

### Page 2

SK하이닉스 (000660) [한화리서치]

2 
특히 전체 생산능력의 50% 이상을 LTA로 확보하면서 나머지 물량은 시장 상황에 따라 탄력적으로 대응할 수 있다는 점
에 주목할 필요가 있음. 장기계약을 통해 안정적인 매출 기반을 확보하는 동시에 미계약 물량을 활용해 추가적인 가격 
상승에 대응할 수 있기 때문임. 물론 장기계약이 메모리 가격 하락을 완전히 방어하는 것은 아니지만, 과거와 비교해 물
량 가시성과 고객사와의 협상력이 동시에 개선되고 있다는 점은 분명함. 과거 메모리 산업이 높은 이익을 창출하는 국면
에서도 낮은 밸류에이션을 적용 받았던 이유 중 하나는 이익의 지속성에 대한 불확실성이었음. 그러나 장기계약 확대와 
공급 제약의 장기화가 동시에 진행된다면 시장이 평가하는 이익의 지속성 역시 높아질 수 있음. 따라서 향후 주가 상승
은 단순한 EPS 추정치 상향뿐 아니라 이익 변동성 축소에 따른 밸류에이션 재평가가 동시에 진행되는 형태로 나타날 가
능성이 높다고 판단함. 
AI 인프라 투자에 대한 우려 역시 중장기 메모리 수요를 훼손할 가능성은 제한적이라고 판단함. 동사는 주요 하이퍼스케
일러의 클라우드 매출이 전년 대비 30~80% 성장하고 있으며 기업용 AI 솔루션 계약 확대와 클라우드 수주잔고 증가를 
통해 AI 투자의 사업적 성과가 확인되고 있다고 설명함. 특히 초기 AI 투자가 소수 빅테크에 집중되었던 것과 달리 최근
에는 일반기업의 자체 인프라 구축, 소버린 AI, 온프레미스 등으로 투자 주체가 다변화되고 있다는 점도 긍정적임. 특정 
사업자의 투자 속도 조절이 전체 AI 인프라 수요에 미치는 영향은 과거 대비 완화될 수 있기 때문임. AI 투자에 대한 우
려가 지속되는 상황에서도 실제 메모리 수요와 고객사들의 장기계약 확대가 확인되고 있다는 점에서 메모리 업황의 구조
적 성장에 대한 긍정적인 시각을 유지함.

HBM4 경쟁력 강화와 CAPEX 확대, 이익 성장과 주주환원의 선순환 
HBM4의 본격적인 출하 확대와 신규 생산능력 확보 역시 2027년 실적 성장의 중요한 기반이 될 전망임. 동사는 M15X
의 생산능력을 연말까지 최대 수준으로 확대할 계획이며 HBM4의 양산 수율 역시 당초 목표를 상회하는 등 안정적인 
생산체계를 확보하고 있음. 특히 HBM 수요가 공급능력의 두 배를 상회하는 상황에서 고객사들의 높은 선호가 이어지고 
있다는 점은 경쟁력 유지에 긍정적임. 최근 일부 고객사들이 HBM 적층 단수를 낮추는 방안을 검토하고 있으나 이는 공
급 부족 상황에서 제한된 물량을 효율적으로 활용하기 위한 전략으로 판단됨. 적층 단수 조정이 전체 HBM 수요 감소로 
직결되는 것은 아니며 오히려 후공정 생산능력에 대한 요구가 확대될 가능성도 존재함. HBM4E와 커스텀 HBM 개발 
역시 고객사 일정에 맞춰 진행되고 있어 차세대 제품에서도 경쟁력을 유지할 것으로 기대됨. 
한편 2027년 CAPEX는 선단 공정 전환과 용인 클러스터를 비롯한 국내외 생산 인프라 확충으로 전년 대비 증가할 전망
임. 다만 CAPEX 확대를 과거와 같은 공급 과잉의 신호로 해석할 필요는 없다고 판단함. 선단 공정의 기술적 난도 상승
과 고부가 제품 비중 확대에 따라 투자금액 증가가 실제 생산량 증가로 정비례하지 않기 때문임. 특히 동사는 신규 팹 
건설 일정을 앞당기면서도 클린룸은 순차적으로 개방하고 실제 생산능력 확대는 최종 수요와 수익성을 고려해 단계적으
로 진행할 계획임. 고객사의 투자 계획뿐 아니라 재무건전성과 자금조달 여력, 공급망 준비 상황까지 점검해 CAPEX 집
행에 반영한다는 점도 긍정적임. 이는 AI 인프라 투자 확대에 대응하면서도 과거 메모리 산업의 공급 과잉 위험을 최소
화하기 위한 전략으로 판단함. 
동사는 이에 더해 2026년 회계연도 기준 약 100조원 규모의 주주환원 계획을 발표했음. 현금배당 30조원 이상, 자사주 
매입 및 소각 60조원 이상으로 구성되며, 기존에 약속했던 잉여현금흐름(FCF)의 50% 이상을 주주에게 환원한다는 원칙
을 구체화한 것임. 지난 8월 발표한 40조원 규모의 자사주 매입은 10월 21일 완료됐으며, 해당 주식은 향후 소각될 예정
임. 이번 3분기 이사회에서는 현금배당 14조원과 추가 자사주 매입 20조원을 결의했으며, 자사주 매입액 중 10조원은 전
량 소각하고 나머지 10조원은 임직원 보상에 활용할 계획임. 임직원 보상용 자사주는 주주환원 규모에서 제외됨. 특히 
향후에도 자사주 매입을 지속할 계획이라는 점에서 주주환원 정책의 지속성이 강화됐다는 판단임.

### Page 3

SK하이닉스 (000660) [한화리서치]

3 
3분기 주당 배당금은 2만원으로, 실적 개선을 반영한 일반배당 1만원과 키옥시아 투자수익을 활용한 특별배당 1만원으
로 구성됨. 총 배당 규모는 약 14조원이며 회사는 올해 실현한 키옥시아 투자수익의 대부분을 3분기 및 연말 특별배당을 
통해 주주에게 환원할 계획임. 이번 결정은 메모리 가격 상승과 고부가 제품 확대에 따른 현금창출력 개선이 실질적인 
주주환원 확대로 연결되고 있음을 보여줌. 특히 2027년 설비투자 확대가 예상되는 상황에서도 대규모 현금배당과 자사
주 소각을 병행한다는 점은 재무적 여력에 대한 자신감을 반영하는 것으로 판단됨. 향후에도 안정적인 실적 성장과 FCF 
확대가 이어질 경우 주주환원의 지속 가능성이 높아질 전망이며 이는 과거 대비 높아진 이익 창출력과 함께 중장기 밸류
에이션 재평가를 뒷받침하는 요인으로 작용할 것으로 예상됨. 
결국 이번 실적발표에서 확인된 핵심은 예상보다 강한 메모리 가격 상승과 HBM4의 본격적인 출하 확대, 그리고 장기계
약을 통한 이익 가시성 개선임. 특히 HBM4의 높은 가격과 안정적인 양산 수율, 기존 DRAM의 가격 상승이 동시에 확
인되면서 2027년 이익 추정치의 추가 상향 가능성이 높아졌다고 판단함. 여기에 LTA 확대에 따른 중장기 이익 안정성 
개선과 대규모 주주환원까지 더해지고 있음. 실적 추정치 상향과 이익의 지속성 개선이 동시에 진행되고 있다는 점에서 
긍정적인 투자 시각을 유지함.

이 자료는 조사분석 담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성됐음을 확인합니다. 
본인은 이 자료에서 다룬 종목과 관련해 공표일 현재 관련 법규상 알려야 할 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 본인은 이 자료를 
기관투자자 또는 제3 자에게 사전에 제공한 사실이 없습니다. (박준영, 김나우) 
저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
이 자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위해 당사 고객에 한하여 배포되는 자료로서 저작권이 당사에 있으며 불법 복제 및 배포를 금
합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 5

SK하이닉스 (000660) [한화리서치]

5 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

자동차업종

◼ 3Q26 비용절감/환율 효과로 우려 대비 양호한 실적 시현 
◼ 2027년 물량–환율 영향 상쇄되며 2H27 이익체력 개선 본격화 예상 
◼ 1) Physical AI(로봇/SDV) 경쟁력, 2) 주요국 점유율 반등에 주목

투자의견 매수 유지, 목표주가 50만원 하향(-6%) 
현대차 TP 50만원(시총 102조원)은 12MF EPS 41,978원에 Target PER 12배
적용하여 산출. 제한적인 실적 기대 반영해 타겟 밸류 하향함에 따라 TP 조정  
우려 대비 양호한 3Q26 실적으로 주가 하방 부담 완화. 주가의 추세적 상승 
위해서는 1) Physical AI(로봇/SDV) 경쟁력, 2) 주요국 점유율 반등 확인돼야 할 
것. 기저효과에 따른 물량 개선이 주가 저점 받쳐줄 것이나, 현재 밸류(2027년 
PER 9.1배vs 글로벌 피어 6.1배)에서 추세 상승 이끌긴 어려울 것이기 때문. 
이에, 자동차 본업 외 1) 한/미국 Physical AI 투자 계획 및 2) BD 공급망 구축 
/양산 가시화에 따른 주가 반등 기대

3Q26 매출 45조(YoY -3%, 이하 YoY), OP 2.7조(+6%), OPM 5.9% 시현 
3Q26 OP 추정(2.5조)/컨센(2.4조) 상회. 파업에 따른 생산/판매 차질 영향으로 
기대 제한적이었으나, 비용 절감/환율 영향으로 우려 대비 양호한 실적 시현. 
[자동차] 매출 33조(-10%), OP 2.1조(+25%), OPM 6.5%. 도매 판매 92.1만대
(-11%). 판매 부진과 원자재 부담에도 불구, 환효과(+1.3조)/비용절감(+0.5조)
로 실적 방어. 이외 YoY 변수별 영향은 물량(-1.1조)/믹스(-0.6조)/원자재(-
0.5조). [금융] 매출 9.2조(+28%), OP 4,710억(-18%), OPM 5.1%, [기타] 매출 
2.9조(+2%), OP 712억(-72%), OPM 2.5% 기록

### Page 5

DAISHIN SECURITIES

5  
  현대차 3Q26 실적 컨퍼런스콜 QnA 
Q. 금융부문 환율 변동에 따른 4Q26 매출 및 손익 영향 
- 금융부문 환율 영향은 현대차 실적에 미치는 환율 영향과 동일한 방향으로 발생

Q. 3Q26 금융부문 수익성 악화 요인 
- 장기금리 상승에 따른 조달 및 이자비용 증가가 주요인. 자산/부채 만기 매칭에 따른 
금리 상승 영향 반영 
- EV 중심 중고차 가격지수 소폭 하락 및 차량 반환에 따른 매각손실, 금리 상승에 따
른 연체율 및 신용비용 상승 
- 차량 판매가격 인하 영향은 없으며, 3Q26 도매 판매 감소에 따른 금융 영업수익 영향 
불가피했음

Q. 향후 주주환원 정책에서 배당 및 자사주 매입 우선순위 
- 기존 밸류업 정책: 최소 DPS 1만원/TPR 35% 제시. 연간 배당 약 2.6조원 → TPR 
35% 충족 위한 잔여분 자사주 매입/소각 활용 
- 경영환경 변화 고려하여 기존 밸류업 프로그램 전반의 주주가치 제고 방안 재검토중 
- 새로운 정책 확정 및 주주 소통 전까지 기존 약속인 최소 DPS 1만원 유지할 계획 
- 시장의 배당 및 자사주 매입 간 균형 요구를 인지하고 있으며, 주주 의견 수렴 후 주
주가치 제고 계획 공유 예정

Q. 3Q26 영업이익 증감 요인 중 기타 +6,400억 발생 원인 
- 월별 컨틴전시 활동을 통한 비용 최적화 및 재료비 절감 지속 추진 
- 4Q26 원가 절감 효과는 3Q26 대비 확대 예상

Q. 3Q26 영업외손익 감소 원인 
- 3Q26 환율 급락으로 외화 매출채권 등 외화자산 관련 외화환산손실 및 외환거래손실 
발생 
- 매출채권 인식 시점과 회수 시점 간 환율 차이에 따른 외환차손 발생 
- 지분법 대상 기업들의 3Q26 실적 부진에 따른 지분법손익 감소도 일부 영향. 대부분 
외환 관련 손실에서 발생

Q. 중국 OEM 국내 진출 확대에 따른 대응 및 신차/가격 전략 
- 중국 OEM 및 테슬라의 국내 M/S 확대 대응중. 다만, 잦은 가격 조정은 중고차 잔존
가치 및 고객가치 훼손할 수 있어 지양 
- 현대캐피탈의 캡티브 금융 경쟁력 활용. 잔존가치 유예할부 등을 통해 월 납입금을 낮
춰 중국 OEM 대비 경쟁력 확보 
- 서비스 네트워크 기반으로 경쟁사 대비 경쟁력 확보 노력 
- 국내 신차: 4Q26 아반떼 HEV, 투싼, GV90, G80 HEV, 싼타페 FL(2026.12월), G90 
FL 등 출시 예정 
- 북미: 4Q26 투싼, GV80 HEV 출시 및 27년 초 GV90 출시 예상 
- 유럽: 아이오닉3 판매 개시, 4Q26 투싼/베이온 등 주요 모델 출시 예정 
- 중국: 아이오닉 V / 인도: 베이온, 아이오닉 P 등 출시 예정 
- 신차 출시 계획 연도별로 다수 예정. 가격은 원가/경쟁사/소비자 가격 수용성 등을 고
려해 결정

### Page 6

현대차(005380)

Q. 3Q26 법인세비용 증가 원인 및 4Q26 전망 
- 일회성 요인보다는 법인별 손익 믹스 및 적용 세율 차이에 따른 영향 
- 4Q26 법인세 수준은 QoQ 큰 변동 없을 것으로 전망

Document 271: AI繁荣背后，美国经济正埋下一颗“资本撤离”炸弹

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI繁荣背后，美国经济正埋下一颗“资本撤离”炸弹.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-20
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

美国经常账⼾⾚字对外国股权资本的依赖升⾄历史⾼位， AI 投资热潮背后隐藏着脆弱的融
资结构。彭博宏观策略师 Simon White 警告，⼀旦 AI 需求降温、科技股下跌，外国资本可
能迅速撤离，推⾼融资成本并引发投资收缩，形成冲击经济的负反馈循环。当前衰退⻛险
尚低，但潜在脆弱性正在累积。
摘要：
美国经济正站在⼀个脆弱的平衡点上。 AI 投资热潮驱动着经济增⻓，但为这场盛宴买单的，越来
越多是随时可能撤离的外国股权资本 —— ⼀旦市场情绪逆转，这⼀融资结构将成为触发衰退的
引线。
据彭博宏观策略师 Simon White 分析，美国经常账⼾⾚字⽬前⼏乎完全依赖对回报敏感的外国股
权资⾦流⼊来填补，这⼀依赖程度已创历史纪录。股权资本不同于债务融资或直接投资，其流
动性强、波动性⾼，是最容易 " 说⾛就⾛ " 的资本形式。⼀旦股价⾛弱、市场情绪转向，今⽇的资
⾦流⼊便可能迅速变为明⽇的资⾦外逃。
当前， AI 直接相关投资单独⽀撑着近⼗分之⼀的 GDP ⽔平，约四分之⼀的名义年增⻓率（ 5.9% ）

来⾃ AI 领域。然⽽，⻩仁勋突然离开英伟达，以及⺠主党在中期选举中的胜利为数据中⼼建设前
景蒙上阴影，正令市场对 AI 需求能否持续产⽣更多疑问。这些不确定性叠加美国经济对外部股权
融资的⾼度依赖，使得潜在的 " 外部融资冲击 " ⻛险正从幕后⾛向台前。
股权融资依赖创纪录，经常账⼾⾚字⾼度暴露
AI 投资规模之庞⼤，已远超美国国内储蓄所能承载的上限。
据 Simon White 分析，外国投资者正在为逾万亿美元的 AI 相关投资提供资⾦，⽽这些资⾦⼏乎全
部以购买美国股票的形式流⼊ —— 这⼀现象在美国乃⾄全球历史上均属罕⻅。
这意味着，美国经常账⼾⾚字的融资来源，已从相对稳定的债务⼯具或外商直接投资，⾼度集
中于波动性极强的股权资本。股权资本的本质决定了其脆弱性：当股价下跌、流动性收紧，资
⾦外流的速度可以极为迅猛，进⽽推⾼资本成本，并对依赖外部融资的科技投资热潮形成直接
打击。
历史上与美国当前处境最为接近的案例，是 2006 年前后的南⾮。据 IMF 数据，彼时南⾮流⼊股
权投资占 GDP 约 5% ，经常账⼾⾚字约为 GDP 的 6.5% ，股权融资覆盖率刚刚超过 75%。随后全

球⾦融危机爆发，资本⼤规模外逃，南⾮股市以美元计价跌去近三分之⼆，货币贬值约⼀半，
并引发了⼀场短暂但剧烈的衰退，百万⼈因此失业。 Simon White 指出，尽管当前并⽆银⾏业危
机迹象，但南⾮的案例清晰揭⽰了美国在股市进⼀步⾛弱或全球其他地区陷⼊衰退时所⾯临的
脆弱性。
英伟达变局与需求隐忧， AI 投资逻辑遭遇考验
⽀撑这⼀融资结构的核⼼前提，是市场对 AI 需求持续旺盛的信⼼。然⽽，这⼀前提正受到多重因
素的侵蚀。
⻩仁勋突然离开英伟达，令算⼒供需图景骤然清晰起来。英伟达此前扮演着 AI 领域 " 事实上的央
⾏ " ⻆⾊，不仅为新兴云计算公司（ neocloud ）及其他算⼒买家提供资⾦⽀持，还为芯⽚融资提
供背书，以此托举⾃⾝产品需求。 Huang 本⼈更常常亲⾃参与英伟达的商业谈判。其离职带来的
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
112 收藏 分享到 :
写评论
图⽚
2 条评论
不确定性，对依赖英伟达⽀持的 CoreWeave 、 Nebius 等新兴云计算公司⽽⾔尤为敏感 —— 这两
家公司合计约占 2027 年预测 AI 资本⽀出的 10% 。
与此同时，⺠主党在中期选举中的胜利，令外界对数据中⼼建设可能遭遇政治层⾯管控的担忧
升温，进⼀步压制了市场对 AI 需求前景的预期。 Simon White 指出，算⼒过剩⻛险早已存在，超
⼤规模云计算商和 AI 实验室对算⼒的 " ⽣存级 " 渴求，曾⼀度将理性经济逻辑抛诸脑后，如今市场
正在为这种⾮理性买单。
营收敏感性上升，股价与投资形成负反馈
在估值层⾯，当前科技股价格的增⻓动⼒已⾼度集中于营收预期，⽽⾮估值扩张。据 Simon
White 分析，追踪营收与预期营收已成为科技股价格增⻓的最⼤贡献因素，与此同时，远期市盈

率正对股价形成制约，远期利润率也开始下滑。
这意味着，科技股对算⼒需求下滑所带来的营收冲击格外敏感。⼀旦算⼒需求⾛弱，营收下降
将直接压低股价；股价下跌则触发外国资本外流，推⾼资本成本；资本成本上升进⽽压缩投资
规模，形成完整的负反馈闭环。值得注意的是，即便约⼀半的 AI ⽀出流向进⼝商品，剩余部分对
国内投资的冲击依然不可忽视。当国内投资收缩、营收转负，衰退的条件便趋于成熟。
Simon White 表⽰，基于其衰退指标，当前爆发迫在眉睫衰退的⻛险仍属较低⽔平，但投资者应
充分意识到上述反馈机制的存在 —— 这些机制⼀旦启动，可能以远超预期的速度将经济拖⼊衰
退。 " 患者表⾯看似健康，但潜在的并发症正在积聚。 "

Document 273: ANT's IPO Is Hot, But Stock Market Gets Cold Feet And Gold Panic Worsens

Source PDF: all report pdf/ANT's IPO Is Hot, But Stock Market Gets Cold Feet And Gold Panic Worsens.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-20
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: ANT's IPO Is Hot, But Stock Market Gets Cold Feet And Gold Panic Worsens
Date:    17 minutes ago
ANT's IPO Is Hot, But Stock Market Gets Cold Feet And Gold Panic Worsens

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
ANT's IPO Is Hot, But Stock
Market Gets Cold Feet And Gold
Panic Worsens
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 19 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Gary Meulemans / Unsplash

I. Anthropic Makes History, Stocks Fall
Today marked Anthropic's stock market debut. It showed that enthusiasm
for AI remains strong, even as broader market sentiment has soured.
Anthropic raised $81.3 billion through its IPO, surpassing SpaceX's $75
billion offering to become the largest IPO in history. Including a concurrent

### Page 2

private placement, the deal raised $107.3 billion. The shares were priced
at $83, valuing Anthropic at $1.5 trillion and making it the world's 12th
most valuable publicly traded company. They opened at $111.50, briefly
pushing its valuation above $2 trillion, before retreating from an intraday
high of $115.10 to $95.05, still 14.5% above the IPO price.
The S&P 500 reversed an early rally this morning to fall 2.0%, while the
Nasdaq dropped 2.2% (charts). Not even the largest IPO in history could

reverse the jitters surrounding the AI trade.
The selloff extended to semiconductor stocks, with AMD plunging 7.7%
after a short-seller report raised concerns about its ties to Chinese
entities, including a US-blacklisted server manufacturer (chart). Coming
on the back of the Nvidia controversy and tighter US export controls, the

### Page 3

report provided another reminder of the political speed bumps facing the
AI trade.
For us, however, the IPO's biggest significance lies ahead. We have
argued that financial, political, and physical constraints may slow the AI
buildout, but are unlikely to halt it as long as demand for compute keeps
growing. Anthropic's future earnings reports will provide a clearer window
into AI adoption across the economy, helping us assess whether demand
for compute is strong enough to sustain one of the US economy's most

important stimulus programs: the AI buildout. We believe it is and continue
to view the recent selloff as a buying opportunity.
We are sticking with our S&P 500 target of 7,900, based on projected
2027 EPS of $425 and a forward P/E of 18.6. Industry analysts continue
to raise earnings estimates, with the consensus 2028 EPS estimate at
$496 versus our $450 forecast (chart). We suspect some irrational
exuberance, as these forecasts fail to account for the AI buildout's speed

bumps.

### Page 4

Investors, meanwhile, are increasingly skeptical of the analysts' optimistic
forecasts. So investors pushed the S&P 500 forward P/E down to 17.6
(chart). That's below last year's Liberation Day low. We think that panic
selling should abate soon.
Today's latest stock market selloff suggests that the two Bull/Bear Ratios
we track will continue to fall below their averages over time (since 1987)
this week (chart). From a contrarian perspective, that would be bullish.

### Page 5

II. US Capital Flows Supporting The Dollar
The latest Treasury International Capital (TIC) data show that foreign
demand for US financial assets remained exceptionally strong through
September. Net foreign capital inflows totaled $1.43 trillion over the 12
months through September, remaining near the record-high $1.82 trillion
last year (chart).
Equities attracted a staggering $944.7 billion, including $787.1 billion from
private foreign investors and $157.6 billion from foreign official institutions

(chart).

### Page 6

The 12-month total for foreign equity inflows has surged over the past two
years to around $1.0 trillion on a 12-month basis (chart).
Private foreign purchases of US equities reached $787.1 billion, nearly
matching the $821.5 billion pouring into US bonds (chart).

### Page 7

Interestingly, corporate bonds attracted more foreign private capital than
Treasuries, underscoring the appeal of US corporate debt (chart).
III. US Economy On A Roll
The robust foreign demand for US financial assets is consistent with the
underlying strength of the US economy. The Atlanta Fed's GDPNow
model estimates Q3 real GDP growth at a solid 3.2% (saar). Consumer
spending is expected to grow at a robust 2.5% pace, while business
investment is projected to surge 9.2% (chart).

### Page 8

The labor market remains strong too, with initial jobless claims at just
203,000 and continuing claims at 1.76 million, both near multi-year lows
(chart).
IV. Gold Keeps Falling Through Support Levels
The gold market is experiencing a selling panic. The recent selloff below
$4,000 per ounce, down to $3,682, was accompanied by record increases
in daily volume in the GLD ETF (charts). We recently pushed our $5,000
target from the end of this year to the end of next year. Our conviction

level has been greatly reduced by the selloff since the start of the Middle
East war this year. We expect that the gold price will find support around
$3,400.

### Page 9

may be featured in the next "Just Ask Dr. Ed!"

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -29.18 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh khoản suy

trong tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -3,013 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã MCH, VIC và FPT; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở mức

4,275 hợp đồng và đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -7,815 hợp đồng.

điểm - giảm khoảng 1,7% so với tuần trước và đang dao động quanh vùng hỗ trợ tâm lý 
1.700 điểm, cũng là vùng đáy cũ của tháng 8. Thanh khoản thị trường tiếp tục suy yếu, 
phản ánh tâm lý thận trọng cao độ trong bối cảnh thị trường thiếu vắng thông tin hỗ trợ 
trong nước, trong khi các yếu tố bên ngoài như xung đột tại Iran, và chi phí vốn toàn cầu 
chưa hạ nhiệt tiếp tục đè nặng tâm lý nhà đầu tư. Khối ngoại duy trì chuỗi bán ròng kéo 
dài nhiều phiên liên tiếp, nối dài đà rút vốn đã lên tới xấp xỉ 95 nghìn tỷ đồng từ đầu năm.

tác giúp thị trường giao dịch cân bằng trở lại.

giảm thứ 4 liên tiếp, cho thấy áp lực phân phối vẫn đang có phần lấn át. Trên khung ngày, 
tuy chỉ số đã có nỗ lực phục hồi trở lại trong 3 phiên đan xen, đặc biệt là phản ứng trong 
phiên cuối tuần khi chạm trendline trung hạn, nhưng việc chỉ có thể hình thành các mẫu 
nến "spinning" hàm ý đà tích lũy chưa thực sự thuyết phục. Trong một vài phiên kế tiếp, 
VNINDEX có thể xuất hiện nhịp hồi kỹ thuật từ vùng hỗ trợ, tương ứng với cạnh dưới tam 
giác, nhưng sau khi chớm phá vùng hỗ trợ quanh 1710 và hiện chỉ còn một số ít các mã giữ 
được xu hướng tăng tính từ đáy tháng 7, xu hướng ngắn hạn đang trở nên khá trung tính.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên) 
Kháng cự xa: 1780 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1740 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1680 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1630 (+-15)

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: cost of living front and centre
By Ryan Wells, Elliot Clarke & Illiana Jain
09:49 November 13 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, the Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Index fell 2.7% to 78.2 in November, its weakest
reading since late-2022 and a level historically associated with periods of severe economic stress.
Renewed cost-of-living and interest rate pressures were front and centre following another lift in

inflation, including sharply higher petrol prices, and the RBA’s November rate increase. Mortgage
holders are feeling the squeeze most acutely, with sentiment across this group falling to a record low.

### Page 2

Consumers have become noticeably more downbeat on the near-term economic outlook – the relevant
sub-index dropping 10.2% to be 22% below its long-run average. Job-loss fears also continue to edge
up.
Businesses are similarly troubled. The latest NAB business survey showed confidence slipped further
to –15 in October as conditions held near their weakest level since the pandemic. Of concern for the
outlook, forward orders fell to –12, also the weakest read since the pandemic, and capital expenditure

intentions deteriorated. Purchase costs continue to grow materially faster than selling prices, further
compressing already tight margins.
Housing provides a clear example of how caution and tighter financial conditions can jolt activity. The
value of new dwelling finance approvals plunged 15% in Q3, the largest quarterly decline in the series’
history, taking approvals 25% lower year-to-date. Investor lending bore the brunt of the adjustment,
falling 30% over the quarter as higher rates combined with the housing tax changes announced in May.

Owner-occupier lending also weakened, however, with first-home buyers proving particularly sensitive
to the deterioration in affordability and market conditions.
Taken together, the data points to a further loss of momentum across the domestic economy,
consistent with Westpac’s expectation for subdued growth and concern over downside activity risks.
Meanwhile, in the US, October’s headline CPI met our expectations but at 0.4% was a touch above the

consensus view. Energy and food both contributed meaningfully to the result, but core inflation was
also marginally stronger than forecast, +0.3%. Core goods inflation has accelerated over the past four
months to average a gain of 0.2% as businesses look to pass tariffs, tech and energy input cost
inflation through. Shelter meanwhile continued to rise at an above-average pace, gaining 0.3% in
October.
The market impact of the October CPI report was accentuated by hawkish remarks from Fed Governor

Waller prior to the release. He expressed confidence in economic activity and the labour market while
showing significant concern over the strength and persistence of inflation, focusing on the passthrough
of energy and technology input inflation. His conclusion for policy was clear: "if, as I expect, the
monthly rate of inflation improves in October, I am leaning toward supporting an increase in our policy
rate of 25 basis points at the FOMC's December meeting"; but "if the October CPI inflation data out

later today are similar to the past two monthly readings—with core CPI inflation around 0.3 percent or
even higher—then a stronger policy move may be needed".
Regional Fed Presidents Goolsbee, Logan and Hammack also kept the focus on inflation risks and
stated or implied the FOMC needs to act. Market participants have responded to this week’s updates,
pricing a 25bp hike in for the December and January meetings, and a cumulative 3.5 hikes by
September. We believe this pricing overstates the need for policy tightening given core inflation ex

shelter remains relatively benign at 2.4% on a six-month annualised basis and the labour market is
balanced; but it will take time for price concerns to subside.
While we agree that the US economy is strong in aggregate, US households are under pressure from
the cost of living, and their investment decision making is constrained by borrowing costs. Highlighting

### Page 3

Related articles
this, US existing home sales fell 5.5% in October after a downwardly revised 1.0% gain in September,
leaving the level of sales below the cycle lows of 2009 and 2010. Our November Market Outlook will
provide a full update on our expectations for the US and the world when released on Westpac IQ this
afternoon.

Document 306: Cliff Notes_ whiplash (2026-11-21)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ whiplash (2026-11-21).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-21
Pages: 11
Status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: whiplash
By Ryan Wells & Elliot Clarke
11:20 November 06 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, the RBA delivered another 25bp rate increase at its November meeting, taking the cash
rate to 4.85%. The move was widely expected, reflecting the Board’s continued concern around
inflation following the recent run higher in global energy prices, firmer domestic capacity pressures and

signs that inflation expectations have edged up. Inflation is now not expected to return to the mid-point
of the target band until mid-2028. This is despite weaker growth expectations and the unemployment

### Page 2

rate trending up to 5.1% by end-2028 on the RBA’s forecasts.
Importantly, though, the decision was not unanimous, with three members preferring to remain on hold
given downside risks to activity and the restraining influence of slower growth. Governor Bullock’s
suggestion that it was time to “take stock” after five rate increases also struck a less hawkish note,
while the statement dropped September’s explicit reference to raising rates further “if needed”. In our

view, February represents the next plausible opportunity for another hike, but we see this only as a risk.
Policy’s effect on housing is becoming increasingly visible. Cotality home prices fell a further 1.2% in
October to now be 6.7% below March’s peak. The cumulative price decline is approaching the scale of
previous housing corrections but appears to have regained momentum after September’s rate
increase. Dwelling approvals saw a 5.1% headline gain in September, but this was driven by volatile

unit approvals while detached house approvals fell sharply, suggesting higher rates and the
established-market downturn are beginning to weigh on the construction pipeline.
Official data on nominal household spending meanwhile rose 0.6% in September and 2.1% for Q3;
however, much of the recent acceleration looks to be price-driven, particularly for fuel. Real spending
volumes expanded by a much more modest (but still solid) 0.7% in Q3.
The Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel provides a granular look at income, spending and saving flows.

The latest update suggests that, beneath the resilient headline story, households are increasingly being
squeezed, particularly those with low savings buffers. With another rate increase now delivered,
housing wealth declining and sentiment weak, Q3 increasingly looks like the high-water mark for
growth in consumer spending.
Lastly for Australia, in September the goods trade surplus widened sharply to $2.8bn, with non-
monetary gold trade yet again adding considerable noise month-to-month. The most notable

development was another exceptionally strong result for data-processing equipment imports, taking the
Q3 total to $11.1bn. This is another clear marker of the scale of Australia’s data-centre investment
boom, which should feature prominently in both business investment and imports in the Q3 National
Accounts.
Offshore, the world has been focused on the US mid-term elections. In the House, Senate and a series
of State Governor races, the Democrats experienced resounding victory. Come 2027, the Democrats

will have a comfortable majority in both houses of Congress and are expected to make use of this
strength to respond to voter concerns, particularly over cost-of-living pressures. The election result also
limits President Trump’s ability to fund an extended military action against Iran. Notably, both
immediately before and after the election, President Trump and the administration have exhibited a
greater interest in furthering negotiations with Iran. It is therefore not a surprise that Brent oil has

backed off its most recent highs around USD118 last Friday, although the scale of the drop to near
USD93 yesterday is arguably too optimistic. Brent has since traded back up to above USD99, still 16%
below Friday’s peak.
While the initial reaction to the US election result was a robust broad-based rally in major equity
indexes, concerns over the potential for tighter regulation of AI and the related infrastructure buildout

### Page 3

Related articles
jolted US equities lower overnight – the Nasdaq falling 5.5% and S&P500 4.4%, both a multiple of the
prior session’s gains. That said, US term interest rates and FOMC rate expectations have also been
scaled back; so, while tech may come under greater scrutiny, a foundation for better outcomes across
the rest of the US economy may become evident in time. US data out this week continued to suggest
their economy is in robust health overall. And, if tensions in the Middle East abate, the FOMC’s

concerns over US inflation should subside. Gaining enough comfort for the FOMC to not hike in
December is a high bar, but the probability of further hikes in 2027 should continue to fall.
Turning then to the UK, the Bank of England raised Bank Rate by 25bps to 4.00% at their November
meeting as expected. On the Bank's baseline expectation, UK inflation is forecast to rise from 3.5%yr
at September to 5.00% by mid-2027. Activity data has also surprised to the upside in recent months,

though "there remains limited evidence of knock-on effects on other prices and wages".
In the press conference, Governor Bailey recognised the nascent improvement in energy price
pressures over the past 48 hours and the effectiveness of monetary policy but also conveyed that
"Unless progress towards resolving the conflict is made, or there is other news which softens the risk to
inflationary pressures, a further increase in Bank Rate may be warranted". Governor Bailey's tone and

comments made in the minutes by other Committee members did not suggest this was a decision that
needs to be made in December, but rather a risk to be judged as additional information is received.
This points to further tightening being more likely in early 2027 than before, and only if conditions / risks
warrant.

### Page 4

2027 ANNUAL OUTLOOK
As we approach 2027, we are faced with two competing trends. Capital, as defined by
interest rates, is delivering higher base returns, so our expectations for required returns to
invest have risen. At the same time the need for this capital continues to grow to deliver the
opportunities, and support the requirements, on the horizon. Companies and governments
are looking to investors to fund the opportunities and priorities ahead, at a time when capital

is more expensive and investment decisions must clear a higher bar.
In last year’s Outlook, we explored how three forces, artificial intelligence (AI), global
fragmentation and inflation, were defining a new market frontier. In 2027, that frontier must
be funded.
Companies need capital to realize the full potential of AI. Governments need capital to
strengthen security and resilience in a fragmented world. At the same time, inflation,
economic growth and central bank policy continue to shape the investment environment.

With cash earning a credible return, investors can afford to be discerning about where they
put capital to work.
That is especially true of the AI build-out. Markets are beginning to question whether the
scale of investment will ultimately pay off. We believe AI is a transformative technology
that will fundamentally change how we live and work. We also believe investors need to be
selective, with a focus on the companies that will define this new frontier.

In this environment, portfolio diversification remains critical. Forward-looking returns for
stock-bond portfolios are the most attractive in years, but market volatility creates a wide
range of possible outcomes. A diversified portfolio and a long-term perspective can help
investors navigate both.
The new market frontier is starting to take shape. As investors consider the opportunities
ahead, we are proud to serve as your financial partner and remain committed to helping you

achieve your long-term goals.
Foreword
David Frame
CEO,  J.P. Morgan Global Private Bank

Scorecard for the 2026 Outlook

### Page 6

6
2027 ANNUAL OUTLOOK
In last year’s Outlook, we highlighted their Promise and Pressure,
and since then the salience of these themes has only grown.
AI offered the potential for higher corporate profits and faster
productivity growth, but also the risk of overinvestment,
exuberance and disruption. Fragmentation favored resilience and
security, but at the expense of cost, efficiency and government
budgets. Inflation had retreated from its post-pandemic peak but

seemed structurally higher and more vulnerable to rolling shocks.
Through 2026, markets moved to reflect the world we envisioned,
in some cases more emphatically than we expected.
Demand for AI continued to far outstrip the supply of AI
computing power (“compute” in the tech sector’s shorthand).
This has rewarded the companies designing, producing and
providing the semiconductors, components and power required
to deploy AI at scale. AI model providers generated real revenue

as businesses began paying up for AI-driven productivity gains.
Companies viewed as vulnerable to disruption underperformed.
Conflicts in Iran and Ukraine continued to highlight deep
vulnerabilities in energy supply chains and defense inventories.
The companies that we believe strengthen economic and national
security resilience outperformed even as traditional defense
companies lagged. Inflation remained above central bank targets,
bond yields climbed to multi-decade highs and a global rate-hiking

cycle kicked into gear.
Against that backdrop, a traditional balanced portfolio seems
set to deliver high single-digit returns. Real assets (such as
infrastructure and commodities) and active strategies (such
as diversified hedge funds) have earned their keep as portfolio
diversifiers.
The three forces (AI, fragmentation, inflation) continue to define
the economic and market environment. And they’re interacting
in sometimes unexpected ways that investors need to understand.

One theme links the three: An enormous demand for capital in both
the private and public sectors is raising the cost of capital. (It’s the
law of supply and demand we remember from Economics 101.)
Investors like you set the price for that capital. That is a position
of strength. It also compels decisions that cannot be deferred.
As many investors look to protect—as well as expand upon—several
years of handsome equity market gains, those decisions become

even more important.
Technology companies need your capital—trillions of dollars’
worth—to fund the next phase of the AI build-out. Governments
need it to finance security, resilience and rearmament initiatives.
Consumers, companies and sovereigns all need it to adapt to an
inflationary, fragmented world.
But inflation, resilient economic growth and central bank policy
mean that cash is more attractive. Today, it earns a real return.
Every financing decision, from a hyperscaler’s data center to a

government’s defense budget to your own portfolio allocation,
now carries an opportunity cost that was obscured for most of
the last cycle.
The AI boom will continue only if investors like you are willing
to provide the financing to bridge the gap between infrastructure
spending and consistent revenue. You will share the cost of
security with governments through taxes, inflation or both, but
you can also decide to own the beneficiaries of that investment.

And a higher cost of capital means every one of these decisions
must now clear a higher bar.
The old investing playbook may not power your portfolio through
the current cycle, even if forward looking returns for traditional
stock-bond mixes are still attractive. In the post–global financial
crisis cycle, policymakers kept the cost of capital low to incentivize
risk taking and boost asset prices. Today, capital has a price. We
believe there is a wider range of possible outcomes and, in the

context of public markets that have rarely been this concentrated,
an increased need to consider exposures you may not have.
Capital is the bridge to a world that is more secure and
productive. Your capital sets the price of progress. Place it wisely.

INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL11
In 2027, central banks will matter again. Before you place your capital, you must make a
judgment about how far central bank policy rates will rise. Inflation is key to that decision
for central banks—and for your portfolio.
We believe that the Federal Reserve and other global central banks are raising rates to
adjust their policy stance and influence inflation expectations. They are not looking to

lower demand. If we are right, the expansion can continue, and our constructive view
on multi-asset portfolios will hold. If we are wrong, the cycle could end with a hiking
campaign that breaks economic demand and pressures risk assets.
While U.S. inflation has remained above the Fed’s 2% target, that reflects supply-driven
factors such as energy prices. The labor market is stable and wage growth is decelerating.
Thus, we believe that inflation will begin to moderate. We see no sign of a nascent

wage-price spiral or overheating economy that the Fed would need to correct with an
aggressive rate hiking campaign.
The European Central Bank (ECB) seems to be running a similar playbook, signaling it will
continue hiking only if it sees further effects of higher energy prices. On the other hand,
the People’s Bank of China (PBoC), facing persistent disinflation, seems likely to keep
policy accommodative for the foreseeable future.
Last year, we argued that we are in a new

regime where transformative technologies
and a changing geopolitical environment
drive investment returns more than
traditional forces such as GDP growth
or central bank policies.

### Page 13

13
INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
13
Inflation containment: Cost of
capital, not the policy rate, is doing
the heavy lifting
In the post–global financial crisis period, unprecedented monetary
policy (zero and negative interest rates and quantitative easing)
coupled with robust forward guidance from the Fed anchored
sovereign rates and compressed credit spreads and equity risk
premiums. In other words, capital had an “artificial” price. Today,

after substantial interest rate hikes, quantitative tightening and
the discontinuation of forward guidance, the price of capital is
more real.
Investors have as much to do with that change as central bankers.
Even before September, when the Fed raised its policy rate 25
basis points (bps), yields were already on the rise. Year to date the
10-year U.S. Treasury has risen roughly 75bps to around 5%. Fifty
of those basis points arrived in September alone, placing the move

in the top decile of monthly increases since 1962. Credit spreads
have widened modestly, investment grade (IG) by around 10bps,
high yield by roughly 40bps.
Importantly, markets are expecting more than two additional
hikes in the current cycle (the next one in December). That means
a higher cost of capital already constrains growth and inflation,
even absent an aggressive Fed.
What are the risks to our view?
What might compel the Fed to kick off an aggressive hiking cycle?

A stronger-than-expected economy, with wages accelerating
ahead of productivity, could force the Fed’s hand. So too could a
sustained move in 5-year/5-year inflation breakevens above 3%.
Indeed, the trajectory of data center construction is important
for bond yields. Ten-year U.S. Treasury yields peaked as investors
began to question further AI capex in 2027. A reacceleration in AI
infrastructure investment could push yields higher.
If higher diesel prices depress economic demand, it would present

a particular challenge to policymakers. In the United States, the
cost of diesel has risen to nearly $6.30/gallon from ~$3.80 before
the Middle East conflict began. Diesel at $10/gallon for a quarter
could make a meaningful dent in economic activity. The Fed would
then have to choose between hiking into a weakening economy
or risking that inflation expectations move higher. This is just one
example of the potential that the next growth downturn could be

accompanied by stubborn inflation, which would pose a challenge
for more traditional portfolios.
INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL

### Page 14

14
INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
Yes, inflation is still a threat
to your capital
Inflation is the foundation for our economic outlook, and we
believe it should also be reflected in the foundation of your
investment plan. Even though we expect inflation to moderate
through 2027, it still poses a key risk to the purchasing power
of your capital.
Many of our clients may also need to use a different benchmark
than policymakers and economists. From 2019 through 2025, the

headline PCE index rose at a ~3.5% annual rate. Over the same
period, based on our own clients’ spending data, average ticket
sizes on luxury goods purchases increased at more than a 6%
rate. Maintaining purchasing power for the goods and services
that matter most to you likely requires a higher hurdle than the
headline inflation index would suggest.

INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
The investment
implications
We believe that fixed income markets are
compensating investors appropriately to take on
the risks of sticky inflation and higher policy rates.
Investment grade yields in the United States are
above 6% for the first time since the global financial
crisis. Long-dated municipals yield roughly 5%,
equivalent to an ~8% taxable-equivalent yield.
At the end of the day, bond investing is math, and today’s bond

math is unusually friendly to investors.
At today’s all-in yields, rates would have to rise roughly another 100bps from here
to wipe out a year of IG carry, a worse outcome than even our bear case. Yields may
not have peaked for the cycle. But we believe that investors who add to high grade
credit today can realize solid returns over the medium term even if global central
banks tighten more than we expect and you experience short-term mark to market

losses along the way.

### Page 16

16
Beyond investment grade, preferred equity looks to be well 
positioned to deliver attractive returns to portfolios. In fact, we 
would rather fund U.S. and Canadian banks through preferred 
equity than common equity. Headline yields are around 7% and 
they are a tax efficient asset for U.S.-based investors. Many of the 
catalysts that could drive bank stock outperformance have played 
out. Still, collecting high-single-digit, tax-efficient returns  
by owning bank preferred stock seems attractive from a risk-
reward perspective. 
Private credit may also be a counterintuitive beneficiary of a rising 
rate environment. Consider: In direct lending, realized loan default 
rates range from 1% to 3%, but a broader “shadow” default rate
1

puts loans under stress at ~15% of the business development 
company (BDC) universe.
2
 Investors are compensated for taking 
on this risk: Spreads including discount at issue have widened 
by about 100bps year-to-date. At a ~5% default rate and ~50% 
recovery, loss-adjusted yields on senior-secured direct lending still 
clear ~7.5%.
3

Higher borrowing costs also present opportunity for distressed 
managers who can charge a high price for liquidity, and we are 
seeing new potential in places such as  private credit secondaries 
and asset-backed finance. 
In a world where capital once again has a price, fixed income is 
where that cost is being paid most explicitly to you.

### Page 17

### Page 18

18
We are more positive on core fixed income than we were last year.
Our long-term outlook for traditional multi-asset portfolios that
mix public equities with core fixed income suggests competitive
returns over the next 10-15 years.
However, we see three reasons to add other diversifiers
to portfolios.
First, in large part due to the uncertainty associated with
the potential payback of AI investment, there is a wide range
of possible outcomes.
Options that protect against a sharp decline in equities are trading

at their highest price of the year. Insurance protection against
default for investment grade companies that are borrowing to
finance the AI infrastructure build is approaching levels more
commonly seen in junk issuers. Assets like diversified hedge fund
strategies tend to perform well when volatility is elevated and
could thus help limit portfolio drawdowns. For example, macro
hedge funds have delivered positive performance on average
during quarters when both stocks and bonds are down.

Second, the threat of inflation shocks driven in part by
geopolitical fragmentation should lead to a positive
correlation between stocks and bonds.
That suggests that a traditional 60% stock/40% bond portfolio
is not prepared for a scenario where stocks and bonds are both
falling. Core real assets and commodities respond to inflation
differently than stocks and bonds. Both deserve the consideration
of your long-term capital.
Third, public equity markets have rarely been this

concentrated, largely because of the dominance of AI and
technology more broadly.
The 10 largest stocks in the S&P 500 account for nearly 40%
of the index, the highest share since at least 1980. Of course,
concentration in technology and AI-linked names has driven
strong performance, which we believe will continue.
Still, investors should consider alternative drivers of capital
appreciation. For instance, U.S. core private equity has a 24%
weight to technology and adjacent companies relative to the

S&P 500 at nearly 50%. In exchange for liquidity, private equity
provides the opportunity for capital appreciation without the
concentration of technology. Manager selection is crucial,
though, especially in an environment where software is at risk of
disruption and leverage-driven returns will be harder to generate.
A balanced portfolio
is only the beginning.

growing demand for capital from

### Page 21

21
Sovereigns need capital to defend themselves in a new era of
conflict, where $70,000 drones can be competitive with million-
dollar missile systems, defense inventories are depleted, and the
U.S. security umbrella is not what it once was.
4
Governments will
increasingly need capital to finance their AI ecosystems (“compute
sovereignty” may become a new catchphrase). And sovereigns
certainly need capital for energy security. Conflict in Iran and the

closure of the Strait of Hormuz were first a shock and then
a sobering reality, and the ongoing conflict has only exacerbated
the fragility of global energy supply chains, to say nothing of the
long-term threats from climate-related risks.
Where will the missing capital come from? You will be
asked to share the cost through some mix of tariffs, taxes,
inflation and higher rates.
Global fragmentation should also force an assessment of
geographic diversification. Be thoughtful about which currencies

you save in, which sovereigns you lend to, and consider the global
spectrum of potential beneficiaries.
Which sovereign blocs have
earned your capital?
Global fragmentation should force you to think more critically
about capital allocation across sovereign blocs. Traditional metrics
such as trade balances and policy credibility are useful, but you
must also consider a country’s natural resources and its exposure
to critical bottlenecks. Can a country defend itself? That question

has new resonance.
We suggest assessing sovereign risks against their vulnerability
to four criteria.
• Conflict. Can the sovereign provide its own security, or is
it dependent on outside support at prices that may prove
unsustainable?
• Compute. Does the sovereign have its own AI infrastructure
and research ecosystem? Is it prepared to defend against
AI-enabled cyberattacks?
• Chokepoints. Can the sovereign rely on domestically run
supply chains, or does it depend on foreign suppliers?

• Climate. Is the sovereign vulnerable to the negative impacts of
climate change, and is it positioned to invest in adaptation?
We think this framework can help identify which countries
and regions might be best positioned for the era of global
fragmentation. The bottom line is that all the blocs that we
care about as capital allocators have vulnerabilities to mitigate.
Concentration in one is probably not prudent.
4    Center for Strategic and International Studies. Drone Saturation: Russia’s Shahed

Campaign.  2025; CSIS. The Cost and Value in Air and Missile Defense Intercepts. 2025.

### Page 22

22
22
First, the United States seems relatively well-positioned to
weather geopolitical fragmentation over the long term, but
there are still critical risks to address. Fiscal capacity, resource
endowment and more limited bottleneck vulnerability all argue
in favor of Treasuries and the dollar. But there is a risk in central
banks and reserve managers diversifying their savings away
from the United States. U.S. policymakers and business leaders
are beginning to address competitive vulnerabilities in areas

such as semiconductor manufacturing, skilled engineering labor
and rare earths. The ongoing El Niño could cause disruption in
insurance markets and food prices, and we are waiting to see
the potential response from insurers and policymakers.
Europe: Market outlooks diverge among equity,
sovereign credit and corporate credit.
While bondholders are funding capital requirements into needed
resiliency spending, stockholders can benefit from that spending.

Europe’s challenges include elevated oil and gas prices
(most European nations are not energy self-sufficient), rising
government bond yields, high levels of debt and more frequent
and severe heatwaves and drought. In France, 10-year government
bonds now yield more than their Italian counterparts for the first
time since the creation of the eurozone, a roughly 130bps swing
from early 2024. (At the end of Q2, France’s public debt-to-GDP
ratio was 119% versus 140% for Italy.) French elections in 2027,

which will likely  produce a contest between candidates on the far
left and far right, loom large.

Here, we make a few observations

### Page 23

23
GLOBAL FRAGMENTATION
European policymakers on the national and pan-European level 
have called for greater investment in defense, technology and 
energy security in lieu of expanded welfare programs. We are 
also monitoring the potential for broader adaptation spending to 
mitigate the adverse impacts of a hotter, less predictable climate. 
German defense spending has doubled since 2022, with Germany 
notably harnessing its industrial infrastructure to diversify away 
from automobile manufacturing.
5
 But the broader push for 
investment has encountered political resistance and limitations  
in scaling up capacity. Only about a third of European Union 
counter-drone and short-range air defense programs are expected 
to be complete by 2030.
6

Given the emerging fiscal strains for European sovereigns, 
we prefer to place our capital in European multinational 
corporations over sovereign debt.
We continue to invest in European equity markets, where 
corporate earnings have proven stronger than expected. Capital 
has flowed to Europe’s beneficiaries of global fragmentation. 
The ranks include the dominant Dutch company that prints 
every advanced chip and French and British defense companies 
capturing rearmament orders.   
Asia: Different blocs, different trades
Geopolitical fragmentation plays differently across Asian markets. 
China is in many ways a beneficiary of the trend. It is an industrial 
and manufacturing power, with an enviable supply of energy. The 
country remains a major exporter (its trade surplus continues 
to set records) and it is building a parallel AI ecosystem with 
sophisticated low-cost models. But economic growth remains 
weak and uneven as its property sector continues to struggle and 
domestic consumption recovers slowly. In September, fixed asset 
investment fell 7.2%.
7

A U.S. or European investor who is considering allocating capital 
to China must also weigh the potential for alignment risk—that is, 
the potential for diverging geopolitical interests with China.  
On balance, we opt for a selective and relatively small allocation 
to Chinese markets, but you shouldn’t ignore it entirely. 
Korea, Taiwan and Japan sell what a fragmented world needs, 
e.g., semiconductors, AI infrastructure and shipbuilding 
equipment. Korea holds roughly 70% of global DRAM capacity, 
while Taiwan dominates advanced chip manufacturing.
8
We remain constructive on Korea and Taiwan equities and 
selectively positive on Japan.  
Latin America: Opportunities for capital in 
nearshoring and commodities
Latin America offers exposure to two related opportunities, the 
nearshoring that is accelerating amid global fragmentation and 
a bull market in commodities.
We see Mexico as a clear beneficiary of nearshoring. The United 
States–Mexico–Canada Agreement (USMCA) remains intact, 
despite political rhetoric from the U.S. administration. Mexico’s 
manufacturing base and deep capital market links to the United 
States underscore the country’s investment potential. That 
potential may increase as firms sell higher value-add exports and 
services into U.S. markets. In fact, tech products now make up 
nearly 50% of Mexico’s exports, and the United States receives 
40% of its imported servers from Mexico.
9
 For investors, it’s 
helpful, too, that the peso is a high-carry convertible currency. 
The rest of the region offers investors a commodity-FX trade: 
Chile and Peru sit on roughly a quarter of the world’s copper 
reserves.
10
 Brazil not only adds iron ore, agriculture and oil, but 
also already hosts 218 data centers, with the potential to double 
capacity by 2030.
11
 As investors in the region, we favor risk assets 
over currencies. We still favor Latin America, even after the Brazil 
election surprise, with a special preference for Mexico and a more 
neutral stance on Chile and Peru.

### Page 24

24
The case for gold and commodities 
In a fragmented world, we believe that gold and other commodities may be  
worth considering as additions to portfolios.
Turning first to gold, many countries have expressed a desire to hold savings in  
something outside the dollar-based system and broadly diversify away from fiat 
currencies.  As a result, central banks have increased their gold holdings as a share  
of overall reserves. The shift hasn’t been uniform, but IMF data shows the dollar’s 
share of official foreign exchange reserves has fallen to roughly 57% from a peak  
of 70% in 2000.
12
Commodities such as copper, oil and gas serve as vital components in compute supply 
chains and national resiliency initiatives. Electrification and grid enhancement help drive 
a projected 25% supply shortfall of copper and 40% shortfall of lithium by 2035.
13

We expect supply shortages to persist even as producers ramp up to meet growing 
demand. New copper mines, for example, take more than 16 years to build.
14
 Importantly, 
too, exposure to commodities offers a portfolio hedge to higher inflation. In fact, over  
the last 50 years commodities have delivered positive returns on average in quarters 
when stock and bond prices both decline.
Despite constrained supply and ongoing structural demand, the stocks of commodity  
and natural resource companies are generally less expensive than the overall market.  
The global energy and materials sectors trade at a 33% and 15% discount to the  
S&P 500, respectively, and sport higher dividend yields.

GLOBAL FRAGMENTATION

### Page 25

25
Do security spending beneficiaries
deserve your capital?
Global fragmentation has supported businesses that benefit from
security spending. The trend will continue, we believe. Indeed,
global fragmentation could be one of the best reasons to
invest globally in equity markets. Most countries face security
vulnerabilities they need to address and most markets host the
companies that benefit from the spending, from Japan and Korea
to France, Sweden and Mexico.

Generally, buyers of security-related products and services
care more about reliability and quick delivery than price. As
we’ve discussed, supply shortages of critical components like
transformers (with lead times between 2.5 and 3 years) and
copper will persist for the foreseeable future.
15
That gives security
companies pricing power and order books that run for years.
Strong earnings (seven of every 10 stocks we follow on this theme
have positive earnings revisions over the last 12 months) and

rising expectations have pushed a basket of security stocks to
a nearly 25% return year to date. We expect continued earnings
growth will drive the next leg of outperformance. Active stock
selection will be critical, though, in separating the market’s
winners and losers and identifying the firms that deserve
your capital.
Where we are placing
our capital
We are placing our own capital behind the need for security
spending in a fragmented world. JPMorganChase’s Security

and Resiliency Initiative (SRI) is a USD 1.5 trillion, 10-year
plan to facilitate, finance and invest in industries critical to
economic security and resiliency.
16
The firm will make direct
equity investments for an initial amount of USD 10 billion in select
companies, primarily in the United States but also in Europe,
to help enhance their growth, spur innovation and accelerate
strategic manufacturing. The remainder of the pledge is designed
to secure debt and loan financing for similar initiatives. Some

recent examples include a financing commitment to MP Materials,
a rare earth metals company, and an equity investment in Shield
AI, a leader in defense technology and autonomous aircraft.
We are also focused on the USD 1.3 trillion upgrade needed for
Europe’s electrical grid.
We are committed to doing our part to address the vulnerabilities
that are inherent in a fragmented world.

2027 ANNUAL OUTLOOK

Part 3

### Page 27

27
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?27
Investors must consider four related issues:
• How far and how fast will AI adoption (early use of AI models)
and diffusion (more extensive use across the economy) progress?
• How many trillions will AI need to deliver to generate returns
for investors?
• Will markets be willing to finance the infrastructure
build-out, and at what price?
• Which layer of the equity stack will be best positioned to generate returns:

the infrastructure layer that builds the AI data centers,
the compute layer that rents out the semiconductors, or the
application layer that turns model tokens (chunks of text)
into durable business revenue?
The AI build-out has created a staggering
demand for capital. Going forward,
political pressure will increasingly
impact the capex boom.
Since 2022, AI capex has totaled over
USD 2 trillion, with trillions more
expected in the next several years.

Will the capex continue?
Yes, if investors like you fund it, and
regulations allow it. And, perhaps more
importantly for long-term investors:
Will AI spending ultimately pay off?
These may be the most important
questions for investors heading into 2027.

### Page 28

28
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?
These four issues inform much of today’s market debate. If you
already own U.S. equities, you have been a passive participant.
That’s because, as our Chairman of Market and Investment
Strategy, Michael Cembalest, has shown, a basket of 54 AI stocks
represents over half of the S&P 500 market cap, 66% of earnings
growth, 59% of the index price return and 82% of capex/R&D
growth since January 2024.
17
We believe in AI’s long-term potential to boost productivity and

corporate profits. But investors are asking serious questions
about the magnitude of the infrastructure build, and markets
may be starting to shift their perception of who the ultimate
winners will be.
A stronger corporate investment cycle is driving higher borrowing costs

Our portfolio positioning reflects a reasonably optimistic outlook.

### Page 29

29
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?29
If AI adoption and diffusion stall, nothing else really matters. The infrastructure  
gets stranded, the compute rents compress and the applications never find their  
potential customers. 
Data tells us that these are still very early days for AI adoption. According to Ramp,  
a financial technology company that tracks business spending, 80% of AI spending is 
driven by 1% of businesses, overwhelmingly technology startups buying tokens from 
other technology startups.
18
 In an analysis of AI and productivity, J.P. Morgan Asset 
Management estimates that only 6% of U.S. work hours are AI-assisted today, even  
as two-thirds of tasks may be theoretically exposed.
19

To justify the trillions of capital targeted for the AI build-out, adoption and diffusion must 
broaden beyond the technology sector and deepen beyond pilot programs. We think this 
will happen, for three reasons.
First, enterprise adoption is shifting from experimentation to productivity. Most large 
enterprises remain skittish about AI agents, which operate with minimal human 
involvement. But as corporate leaders grow more comfortable with the technology, 
we think they will increasingly deploy agentic models that will boost productivity and 
compute intensity per user.
Second, the history of transformative technologies tells us that falling prices unlock 
use cases faster than they compress revenue. Since 2023, the price of a given level 
of AI capability has fallen at a 13x annual rate, which is four times faster than DNA 
sequencing.
20
 Over the same period, annual run-rate revenues for the leading model 
providers have grown from zero to well over USD 100 billion.
21
 Goldman Sachs projects 
a 24x increase in token consumption through 2030 as use cases expand.
22
 J.P. Morgan 
Asset Management projects that overall AI adoption could rise to 40% of today’s tasks by 
2040.
23
Third, consumer agents, which can autonomously handle a range of tasks, could 
gain traction. Meta’s Muse has emerged as one contender in this arena. In many 
large economic sectors like healthcare and transportation, AI models still have a low 
penetration rate, and that too could change.
24

The bear case for AI adoption is straightforward: AI remains a productivity tool for 
technologists and a novelty for everyone else. The error rate remains too high and 
enterprise adoption stalls while most consumers decide to take a pass on the new 
AI spreads through  
the economy—or  
all bets are off

Public and political opposition to AI is a key risk to the larger ecosystem. Data center

### Page 31

31
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?31
AI spending only pays off if the economy generates enough 
revenue from it, and does so quickly enough, to cover the cost of 
the capital deployed (especially data centers). Given the sheer size 
of the current pipeline, and anxiety about how much of it moves 
from plans to completed projects, we expect growing investor 
pressure to see that revenues do in fact materialize.
To understand the challenge, consider what it takes to break even 
or pay for itself year to year, including the cost of investor capital. 
A gigawatt of AI data center capacity costs roughly USD 40 billion. 
To cover its cost of capital, a 1GW facility must generate about 
USD 11 billion in revenue per year.
26
  The ~129GW of global frontier 
data center capacity expected by 2029 would therefore need to 
generate about USD 1.5 trillion a year in compute revenue, largely 
sourced from the application companies that must rent computing 
power to produce products and services that are consumed by 
enterprises, consumers and governments. These companies, 
in particular the private model labs, likely need to earn 50% 
margins above their computing costs. That means end-customer 
AI spending would need to total roughly twice that amount, about 
USD 3 trillion per year. 
So how much revenue could the AI ecosystem earn in the early 
2030s? Under reasonable assumptions, we estimate:
• Enterprises could spend about USD 2.5 trillion a year on AI 
through employee AI tools and agents.
• Consumer sectors could add about USD 750 billion in revenue 
through advertising and subscriptions.
• Governments could add another USD 400 billion.
27

That gets AI revenue to about USD ~3.5 trillion, about USD 700 
billion above the breakeven rate needed for the data centers to 
pay for themselves year to year. That surplus represents returns 
for investors in the compute and application layer. It may seem 
sizable, but it is based on an addressable market that is merely an 
estimate and far from assured.
A closely watched risk is whether a large share of AI-driven 
revenue, especially from large enterprises, comes from job 
displacement—replacing workers, rather than creating new 
How many trillions  
does AI need to deliver? 
demand. If half of potential enterprise AI spending replaces roles 
that would otherwise be filled, and AI costs about half as much 
as the worker it replaces, AI would absorb roughly 24 million 
knowledge worker jobs worldwide over five years, or about 1.3% 
of the global knowledge workforce each year. That is disruptive, 
but it is about a third of the pace at which U.S. manufacturing shed 
its workforce in the 2000s (~3.4% a year) amid factory automation 
and China’s entry into the World Trade Organization. Much of the 
AI adjustment could come through roles left unfilled rather than 
layoffs. The bigger risk is concentration: Displacement that falls 
heavily on specific occupations or regions could test political limits 
well before the aggregate numbers do.
28

To illustrate, if two-thirds of those displaced roles were in the 
United States, the U.S. would absorb roughly 16 million workers 
over five years, or about 3 million a year. That is roughly 2% of 
payrolls annually, more than four times the pace of the 2000s 
manufacturing decline. It would approach the rate of job loss 
during the Great Recession, only sustained for five years rather 
than two. Even if most of AI job displacement avoids layoffs, it 
would at the very least eliminate  the economy’s normal annual 
job growth. As the midterm election results suggest, it seems very 
unlikely that there would be political tolerance of this outcome. 
J.P. Morgan Asset Management’s analysis of AI’s impact on the 
economy suggests that job disruption is more likely to occur within 
jobs rather than eliminate jobs outright. After all, every job is 
ultimately a collection of tasks, and to date, AI can automate or 
augment only a portion of them. Moreover, while we anticipate AI 
diffusion across the economy will be profound, it will take time, 
and adoption is expected to be uneven and gradual. In our view,

       adds up to about USD 11 billion of annual revenue.

this steady uptake should help limit the risk of widespread
job displacement.
Turning back to the economics of the AI build-out: The gap
between the revenue required for the AI ecosystem to break even

and the potential revenue from sources such as enterprises and
consumers is a critical variable. When the potential market is far
larger than what the AI ecosystem needs to break even, every new
data center looks like a solid investment. But as the infrastructure
pipeline grows toward the size of the addressable AI market, the
gap shrinks, and so do the incentives to keep building.
We believe the infrastructure pipeline is entering a more
selective phase. First, the addressable market is still very much

debated, especially after frontier model lab revenues decelerated
recently. Second, bond markets have started to balk at excessive
leverage and aggressive infrastructure plans from smaller AI
players. Finally, overbuilding is often how capital investment
booms end. The railroads and the internet were both tremendously
productive innovations. But far too much track and fiber were laid
before demand arrived. As a result, capital markets suffered severe

losses. In the AI cycle, though, more restrictions around data center
development reduce the risk that capacity exceeds demand.
Going forward, companies will find it difficult to convince
investors that they need additional data center capacity beyond
the current pipeline especially in light of the likelihood of a
more onerous regulatory environment. Therefore, value may
increasingly accrue to companies that control the crucial
bottlenecks, or have the pricing power to work through constraints

such as political pressure, regulatory shifts, power availability, grid
access, permitting speed and financing.

### Page 33

33
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?33
As even casual observers of the AI story understand, financing will be key to
how the plot unfolds. As a result, before you underwrite the equity at any layer
of the AI ecosystem, you should be comfortable with the debt on a company’s
balance sheet. (Another lesson from Economics 101: In a debt default, equity
value disappears.)
Some AI companies have recently hit the limits of what public debt markets

will tolerate. Consider the example of unsecured bonds from CoreWeave,
a “neocloud” (typically highly leveraged providers that rent out their compute
capacity). Their bonds trade at distressed level spreads well over 1,000bps.
Does this signal the beginning of the end of the AI boom?  No, but it does
suggest a different opportunity set within public and private markets.
Although AI debt is growing (AI-related issuance will top USD 300 billion this

year), debt growth as a share of GDP is still much lower than previous historical
leverage cycles and more political hurdles to data center development could
also result in a slower pace of leverage growth.

### Page 34

34
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?
Today, most of the AI debt is issued by investment grade borrowers with some of the
strongest balance sheets in the world. The hyperscalers as a whole (Alphabet, Amazon,
Microsoft, Oracle) carry interest coverage above 50x, compared with ~9x for the
median investment-grade issuer. Oracle is clearly an outlier. We still see opportunity in
some investment grade hyperscaler debt.
New financing structures have drawn scrutiny, though, because they can obscure who

owns the debt and what collateral sits behind it. Case in point: ~USD 165 billion in off-
balance-sheet from Nvidia support roughly 6.5GW of planned compute capacity from new
model providers.
29
Nvidia aims to cultivate a customer base beyond the hyperscalers and
frontier model labs. In principle, this should reduce concentration risk
in the AI ecosystem. But off-balance-sheet structures add to the opacity of the system
and are likely raising the cost of capital.

Artificial intelligence has shifted the technology sector from an asset-light to an asset-
heavy business model. A decade ago, the largest cloud companies reinvested about 7%
of their revenue in capital spending. This year, analysts’ consensus expects roughly 36%.
That is nearly the reinvestment rate of a regulated utility, and it absorbs almost all of the
cloud companies’ operating cash flow.
The further you move from the hyperscalers, the more asset-heavy the business model

becomes. Neoclouds such as CoreWeave spend close to USD 3 in capex for every dollar
of revenue, and they fund the difference with debt. The demand for capital is also growing
in the power sector, where U.S. utilities plan nearly USD 1.5 trillion of investment over the
next five years.
30
Today the assets in focus are GPUs and data centers. Tomorrow they could be
autonomous vehicles and robots. Either way, asset-heavy models need financing. We have

been exploring opportunities to provide that financing through private markets to data
center developers and neoclouds that are struggling to raise capital in public markets on
attractive terms. This is a riskier proposition, which comes with commensurately higher
potential returns.
